                                                               2
Abstract
This document is the first in a series of two (2) reports commissioned by
the Office of the Governor of California1 to provide an independent analysis
of the devastating fires that caused significant destruction to Southern
California between January 7–31, 2025.

During this time, the Southern California region, including large portions of
Los Angeles County and the southern area of Ventura County, experienced
multiple rapidly evolving wildfires and two (2) major conflagrations fueled
by extreme winds, low relative humidity, and drought-stricken vegetation,
leading to 322,3 confirmed fatalities as of September 29, 2025, widespread
destruction, and large-scale community impacts. UL Research Institutes’
Fire Safety Research Institute conducted an extensive data collection effort
to establish a timeline and fact base around these fires.

This report, the Southern California Fires Timeline Report (Phase One),
includes preparedness and initial response activities for the Palisades
and Eaton Fires. The time period covered begins just prior to the start of
the Palisades Fire on January 7, 2025, at 10:30 a.m. PST and concludes
when fire progression through the built environment lessened and when
additional resources were available to address the existing threat for both
fires on the morning of January 8, 2025. This first report goes into detail
about the fires in Palisades and Eaton and summarizes 10 additional fires
that occurred in Southern California during this timeframe.

The Phase One report does not include an analysis of recovery efforts or
the fire’s origin-and-cause investigations, which are being conducted by
the jurisdictional fire departments with assistance from federal and state
agencies and private contractors.

The second report, the Southern California Fires Incident Analysis Report
(Phase Two), will provide a detailed analysis of prevention, preparedness,
and response efforts that include evacuation and fire suppression operations.




Keywords: Palisades Fire, Eaton Fire, wildland urban interface, wildfire, WUI, urban conflagration, California wildfires

1.0 INTRODUCTION


Over the last days of 2024 and throughout the first
week of January 2025, meteorologists responsible
for predicting severe weather in Southern California
began issuing forecasts for extreme conditions and
elevated fire danger across the Los Angeles basin.
By the morning of January 7, 2025, meteorologists
and experienced fire behavior analysts confirmed
the development of high winds and critical burning
conditions that can elevate wildfire risk in the area.

Southern California is accustomed to significant
fires of all types: structural, wildland, wildland-
urban interface (WUI), brush, grassland, alone
or in combinations. Similarly, extreme weather
conditions are common throughout the region,
with strong winds being a regular feature of life             These 12 fires would ultimately claim 32 lives. The
for Los Angeles residents. Southern California fire           two (2) most damaging, the Palisades and Eaton
departments are widely considered among the best              Fires, caused an estimated $76 billion to $131
resourced, most professional, and best prepared               billion in total property and capital losses.4
among their peers across the United States.

risks to their jurisdictions, secured additional              through affected neighborhoods.

in their emergency operations centers (EOCs).                 Given the unprecedented magnitude of this fire

warnings about the potential for extreme weather              comprehensive, independent review of the January
and fire danger to their communities.                         2025 fires in Southern California.

2.0 BACKGROUND


The Southern California region has an extensive                 The area’s built environment also further intensifies
network of organizations that exist to address                  wildfire risk. Hillside development into the WUI has
wildfire prevention, preparedness, and response.                expanded significantly since World War II, resulting
These organizations are funded and managed by                   in a patchwork of neighborhoods with differing
federal, state, and local government entities. Non-             construction methods and materials.
governmental and private organizations also exist to
address the widespread threat of wildfire in the region.        In addition, annual Santa Ana wind events — strong,

Additionally, the region’s coastal topography

range runs from Los Angeles County to San
Bernardino County, with a peak elevation of 10,069
feet, while the Santa Monica Mountain range
extends east to west from the Hollywood Hills in Los
Angeles County to Pont Mugu in Ventura County
with a peak elevation of 3,111 feet.

2.1.2.1 Precipitation
Southern California receives most of its rainfall in
the winter months between November and April,
averaging 13-17 inches. Annual rainfall in the region
can be highly variable, influenced by Pacific Ocean El
Nino/La Nina patterns and other climate interactions,
with some years bringing abnormally high precipitation
followed by years of very low precipitation.

This variability is partly influenced by storms in the

                                                                         26
2.0 BACKGROUND


Mountain wave winds can develop when strong
Santa Ana winds flow from the northeast and are
forced over the San Gabriel Mountains. As the
stable, fast-moving air descends the leeward (south-
facing) slopes, it can create powerful, turbulent
wave patterns known as mountain waves. These
waves often generate intense, gusty surface winds
and dangerous rotor turbulence below the crests,
particularly impacting foothill communities like
Pasadena, Altadena, and Sierra Madre.

The interaction between the strong Santa Ana flow
and the mountainous terrain amplifies wind speeds
and can lead to sudden gusts capable of downing
trees, damaging infrastructure, and posing hazards
to aviation.

Figure 2.1.4 shows high- and low-pressure systems
creating a gradient that causes the air to flow from
the Great Basin region to the area of low pressure
off the Southern California coast.




                                                            Figure 2.1.4 Southern California Santa Ana winds created by the
                                                            confluence of a high-pressure system over the Great Basin and a
                                                            low-pressure system off the Southern California coast.

2.1.6.1.1 Regional Fire History: Palisades                                   In late fall and winter, Santa Ana winds sweep in
The Palisades and surrounding Santa Monica                                   from the northeast, stripping moisture from the
Mountains have endured a near-continuous pattern                             vegetation and converting the landscape into a
of high-impact fires, as seen in Figure 2.1.7 and                            highly flammable corridor. This convergence of
Table 2.1.3, illustrating the challenges associated                          topography, fuel load, and wind have made the
with urban development in a fire-adapted landscape.                          Santa Monica Mountains host to some of the most
                                                                             intense and destructive fires in California’s history.
Spanning 52 miles east to west, the Santa Monica
Mountains are the only mountain range in the U.S.
to bisect a major metropolitan city. Its ecosystem
supports dense chaparral vegetation and complex
drainage networks. During most of the year, moist
coastal flow sustains lush vegetation.

 Bobcat Fire60, 61          2020         115,997     169             0                     Originating during a regional heatwave, the fire caused

                                                                                           wildfires during the 2020 catastrophic fire season.

                                                                           2.2.1 Drinking Water
                                                                           Drinking water in the U.S. may be used for many
                                                                           things, including human consumption, showering/
                                                                           bathing, flushing toilets, washing clothes, watering
                                                                           plants and lawns, and filling swimming pools. As
                                                                           with most areas in the country, drinking water is also
                                                                           the primary source of water supply for firefighting in
                                                                           urban Southern California.

Legal and Regulatory Oversight
There are several government agencies that provide legal and regulatory oversight for wildfire safety in
California. They enforce environmental laws, adopt minimum building and fire code standards, and review
utility wildfire mitigation plans. Table 2.3.4 lists organizations who provide wildfire-related legal and regulatory
oversight for the state.

2.5.2.1 Fire Mutual Assistance Grants
The federal government, through FEMA, currently
provides a Fire Mutual Assistance Grant (FMAG),
which covers 75% of qualifying expenses for
potentially disastrous fires that are actively
threatening significant structural developments.

4.2 Pre-Fire Events

    Sections 4.2 Pre-Fire Events and 4.3 Fire Events include references to various different fire
    departments. To ensure clarity, as well as consistency with Appendix B-1 of the California
    Agency Designators by Agency in FIRESCOPE Field Operations Guide 2022 edition, three
    (3) letter identifiers (highlighted below) will be used throughout these sections.

    • Angeles National Forest (ANF)
    • Arcadia Fire Department (ARC)
    • CAL FIRE (CFD)
    • Los Angeles County Fire Department (LACoFD = LAC)
    • Los Angeles Fire Department (LAFD = LFD)
    • Orange County Fire Authority (OCFA = ORC)
    • Pasadena Fire Department (PFD = PAS)
    • San Marino Fire Department (SNM)
    • Ventura County Fire Department (VCFD = VNC)
    • Mixed Agency Strike Team from Los Angeles County Operational Area A, B, C, E, F, G (XL_)
    • Mixed Agency Strike Team from Orange County (XOR)
    • Mixed Agency Strike Team from Ventura County (XVE)

4.2.2 Staffing and Preparedness
Emergency response agency preparedness can include augmenting staffing and prepositioning resources in
response to forecasted weather and in anticipation of wind-driven fire events and potential evacuation challenges.

Each agency determines the level of preparation they need to execute, which is often related to state and
federal preparation actions. This section includes the preparation activities of relevant agencies before the first
fire, Sunset 1, on January 7, 2025, at 10:09.

4.2.2.2 Local Preparedness
The preparedness activities of utilities and emergency                         At the company level, participation and
response organizations are generally in reaction to                            preparedness varied by station location. Each
the forecasted weather. This section outlines the                              company monitored current and predicted weather
preparedness efforts of fire and law enforcement                               conditions using traditional media sources,
agencies as well as water and electrical utilities. The                        department-issued weather information, and
preparedness actions leading up to January 7, 2025,                            dedicated weather and fire-weather applications and
varied by departments and agencies.                                            websites. Companies verified that all personal and
                                                                               department-issued wildland gear was accounted for,
4.2.2.2.1 Fire Department Preparedness                                         operational, and deployment-ready.

4.2.2.2.1.1 Los Angeles City Fire Department
Preparedness
In the lead up to January 7, 2025, LFD executive
officers followed the staffing plan created by the
city’s senior officer. The department chose to not
hold the off-going shift on the morning of January 7,
2025, a decision that would have had to be made no
later than 06:30 on January 7, 2025.

This data provides multifaceted evidence of the speed and direction the fire moved through the Palisades and
Eaton burn areas and details the response effort of agencies playing a role in handling the emergency.

The weather in the areas of the Palisades and Eaton Fires was measured by RAWS, SCE weather stations,
and weather stations independently owned and recognized by the CWOP.

Satellite images were not used to establish time and location of the fire progression for the Palisades and
Eaton Fires within the Phase One timeline. Fire Guard satellite images will be used within the Phase Two
report if the shape files (i.e. perimeters), the process of fire perimeter creation (i.e. how the satellite sensor
data is used to prepare the perimeters), and the resolution of the images (i.e. pixel size corresponding to
square area of ground surface) are shared with ULRI.

4.3.1 Fire Progression
A detailed accounting of fire progression timestamps and facts impacting the progression of the Palisades and
Eaton Fires are presented in this section. Each fire is assessed independently, starting with the Palisades and
followed by Eaton. Early and late fire progression timestamps for the other 10 fires that occurred in Southern
California during this time will also be included.




    Data Collection Note
    The fire’s movement within these fire perimeters were tracked using timestamped records
    of alarm activations, 911 calls, and emergency radio traffic, as well as pictures and videos
    provided by residents and emergency responders. Additional information was gathered from
    TDs with first responders and local weather station data.

    While this data may not provide unequivocal evidence of when structures and other areas
    within the built environment were impacted by the fire, it does provide an approximate time
    of when increased fire activity reached a general area or region.

4.3.1.1.2 Palisades Fire Progression
The Palisades Fire was first reported via a 911 call
at approximately 10:30 on Tuesday, January 7,
2025, in the area of Skull Rock on the Temescal
Canyon trail, approximately 1,400 feet above sea
level. Driven by Santa Ana winds, the fire spread
over 2.5 miles to the Pacific Coast in less than three
(3) hours. Both wind and topography contributed to            Figure 4.3.3 Aerial view of the Getty Villa showing PCH and South

                      After the first reports of the fire were received at approximately 10:30 on January 7, 2025,
                      LFD dispatched units to the area of North Piedra Morada Drive and West Monte Hermoso
                      Drive for a brush fire. The initial 911 call stated the fire was burning in the hills and heading in
                      the direction of the caller’s home (Figure 4.3.7).

                                                        104
4.0 TIMELINE OF EVENTS                                                                                         PALISADES



A photograph taken at 11:09 from the backyard of a home along Chastain Parkway shows the fire spreading
cross slope SSW (Figure 4.3.12). As the fire spread through the area’s vegetation and built environment, it was
influenced by both wind and topography. Terrain features, such as slope and drainages, can affect wind, both
shielding the surface fuels from its effects and channeling its flow to increase the speed of fire spread.

On the eastern flank, LFD units — Structure Defense Group (SDG) 1 — indicated at 11:08 that about
15 homes on Floresta Place in Ridgeview County Estates were threatened by the fire. At the same time,
a Marquez Knolls resident took a photo (oriented northeast) showing vegetation on fire and the fire line
spreading east.

At this time, the fire had not spread far enough east to come down into the El Medio Bluffs neighborhood
(Figure 4.3.13).




                          Figure 4.3.12 Panoramic image from multiple photographs taken at 11:09 from the backyard along 18**
                          Chastain Parkway East looking northeast depicting the fire spreading down and cross slope.




                          Figure 4.3.13 Photograph provided by a resident taken at 11:25 from 11** Tellem Road (oriented
                          northeast). The fire is spreading southeast in vegetation above the Ridgeview Country Estates community
                          near the Temescal Ridge Trail.



                                                           105
4.0 TIMELINE OF EVENTS                                                                                          PALISADES




    Weather Conditions
    During TDs, an LAFD captain recalled the wind was blowing to the east from his vantage
    point on Floresta Place. This may be an effect of the terrain on wind direction and/or the
    direction of fire spread being influenced by the availability of fuel.




By 11:25, the fire had already jumped to the west side of Palisades Drive. Multiple units confirmed at 11:27
that the fire had spread past Palisades Drive and to the west with some records as far south as Paseo
Miramar by 11:29. Within the first hour, the fire spread over a mile south towards the ocean.

The first reports of structures on fire came from Lachman Lane, starting at 11:27 through 11:30.

“Two homes actively involved. Off Lachman just south of the fire road”, “the house is fully involved, we’re
protecting exposure now. We could use three engine companies.”

Fire continued spreading through vegetation and began spreading into the built environment, impacting structures
around Lachman Lane and Charmel Lane at 11:43. A photograph shows a spot fire (ember-ignited vegetation) in
the vegetation between Turquesa Lane and Lachman Lane (Figure 4.3.14).




                           Figure 4.3.14 Photograph taken at 11:22 of burning vegetation on a hillside behind Lachman Lane.




                                                            106
4.0 TIMELINE OF EVENTS                                                                             PALISADES



                        [11:30-12:29] One hour after start of Palisades Fire
                        One hour after the first report the fire had spread south towards the coast through the
                        Ridgeview Country Estates and Marquez Knolls neighborhoods and west of Palisades Drive
                        (Figure 4.3.15). At 11:40, units were engaged further south in defending structures in the
                        area of Paseo Miramar and Via Lucia on the west side of Palisades Drive.

                        A 911 call reported spot fires in Los Liones Canyon at 11:55, just west of the
                        Castellammare neighborhood. Radio communications from the LAPD’s After Action Report
                        (AAR) on the Palisades Fire indicate the fire was moving near Fire Station 23 (FS23) —
                        the initial incident command post (ICP) — at Los Liones Drive and West Sunset Boulevard
                        around the same time.




Figure 4.3.15 Map of fire activity from 11:30 through 12:29.




                                                               107
4.0 TIMELINE OF EVENTS                                                                                          PALISADES



At 12:08, a video taken (oriented west) at Palisades Drive near the entrance of the Sea Ridge Condominiums
shows vehicles evacuating southbound. At this point, the fire was high on the ridge west of Palisades Drive
near the Paseo Miramar Trail, burning surrounding vegetation.

The fire appeared to be beyond the ridge in some areas. The video provides the earliest reported visual
evidence of fire on the west side of Palisades Drive (Figure 4.3.16).

Further west, radio transmission reported the Getty Villa was being impacted at 12:27 followed shortly by a 911
call at 12:28 reporting the Getty Villa was being impacted on the north end.




                          Figure 4.3.16 Panoramic image from a video taken at 12:08 near the entrance to the Sea Ridge
                          Condominium depicts burning vegetation and established fire on the west side of Palisades Drive.

                       [12:30-13:29] Two hours after start of the Palisades Fire
                       By 12:30, firefighters were defending structures near the area of origin, while the fire continued
                       to spread south along Palisades Drive (Figure 4.3.17). The western flank of the fire began
                       extending through Paseo Miramar and Castellammare neighborhoods. The fire’s eastern flank
                       continued to skirt the interface of Ridgeview County Estates and El Media Bluff.

                  Ridgeview County Estates/Marquez Knolls
                  The head of the fire continued to widen and spread SSW into the built

                  of the Ridgeview County Estates neighborhood, along the eastern flank
                  of the fire.

                  impacting the Castellammare neighborhood. At 13:22, a house located
                  on 7** Paseo Miramar, west of Palisades Drive and northeast of the Getty
                  Villa, was reported to be on fire.

                        [13:30-14:29] Three hours after start of the Palisades Fire
                        The fire’s spread continued towards the ocean, with its eastern flank reaching Temescal
                        Canyon and its western flank moving into the Castellammare neighborhood (Figure 4.3.20).
                        Structure defense by firefighters in the Ridgeview Country Estates helped reduce further fire
                        spread within the built environment in this area.

                        The FIRIS aircraft Intel 12 arrived over the fire around 13:40 and began to collect IR data,
                        which was used to produce the first official perimeter at 14:11 (Figure 4.3.20).

                        the Paseo Miramar and Castellammare neighborhoods. To the east, the fire had spread to
                        Temescal Canyon and into the built environment of the Ridgeview County Estates and the
                        north side of El Medio Bluffs.

                        [15:30-16:29] Five hours after start of the Palisades Fire
                        Over the next hour, the fire continued to widen and spread towards the coast (Figure
                        4.3.33). To the west, structures in Castellammare, Pacific View Estates, and Sunset Mesa
                        neighborhoods were burning and spot fires were seen across Topanga Canyon. On the
                        eastern flank, spot fires had also ignited in the Rivas Canyon neighborhood, with the fire
                        spreading down Temescal Canyon to PCH.

  Weather Conditions
  Between 16:00 and 17:00, sustained winds in the area were 40-50 mph with gusts up to
  62 mph. Winds were originating from the NNE, generally blowing SSW. At Topanga Beach,
  there was a wind reversal at approximately 16:20 with winds coming from the south
  (ocean breeze).




                             Highlands/Ridgeview County Estates/Marquez
                             Knolls
                             LAC units were operating along Shadow Mountain Drive near Via Cresta
                             protecting structures from fire at the interface. Vegetation was burning on
                             the west side of Fire Road, while structures on Lachman Lane continued
                             to burn. At 15:58, firefighters responded to a report of persons trapped on
                             Via Anita.




                             El Medio Bluffs
                             Further east in the El Medio Bluffs neighborhood, LAPD AAR indicates at
                             approximately 15:37 the fire was spreading down Temescal Canyon past
                             West Sunset Boulevard. At 16:17, LAC units reported the fire was burning
                             on both sides of Temescal Canyon Road down to PCH (Figure 4.3.34).

                             To the north at the interface with the foothills, firefighters were experiencing
                             heavy embercasts while defending structures along El Medio Avenue.

                             Further south near PCH, firefighters responded to reports of threatened
                             structures in the Palisades Bowl Mobile Estates and Tahitian Terrace
                             Mobile Home Park between 16:11 and 16:20.

  Weather Conditions
  Temperature measured at the
  Topanga Beach SCE weather
  station spiked to over 100°F around
  16:30, followed by a rapid return to
  previous temperatures.

  These acute increases in air
  temperatures are not high enough to
  indicate direct flame contact with
  the weather station, but may indicate
  sustained burning in the area and/
  or hot gases from fire plume blowing
  towards the measurement location.

                  Fire continued to spread east through Rivas Canyon Park, north of
                  Alphabet Streets. A private weather station on Oracle Place recorded
                  a spike in temperature and wind speed around 17:30 (Figure 4.3.45)
                  consistent with nearby fire activity. Temperature and average wind speed
                  decreased after the peak until the weather station stopped recording data.

                  To the north in the wildland, firefighters reported at 17:52 that the fire was
                  in Sullivan Canyon and suggested that they may need to go wider on the
                  control line.

                  Street. There were also multiple fires reported along Carey Street. By

  Weather Conditions
  At 19:30, the weather station near Topanga Beach reported sustained wind speeds of 18 mph
  with gusts up to 35 mph coming from the SSW and blowing NNE, with the temperature at
  76°F and relative humidity at 14%.

  Around the same time, a separate weather station located off Tuna Canyon Road near Las
  Flores Canyon Heights Road reported 14 mph sustained winds with gusts up to 27 mph,
  originating from the SSE and blowing NNW. Further west, the Malibu Hills RAWS reported
  sustained winds at 45 mph with gusts up to 67 mph from the NE, blowing SW.

  NOTE: The differences in measured winds and temperatures are caused by microclimates, the
  effect of terrain on wind flows, and other factors that will be explored in the Phase Two report.

                                Living needed to be evacuated.

                                of the Summit neighborhood with estimated wind speeds of 75-80 mph
                                coming from the south and blowing north. Firefighters were engaged in

                                [degree angle] and every single one was on fire.”

4.0 TIMELINE OF EVENTS                                                                    EATON




  Weather Conditions
  Weather station data supports the AC’s perception of the wind. From the beginning of the fire
  incident, it was reported that wind on Henninger Flats Road was coming mostly from NNE-NE,
  while Rubio Canyon was mostly from NNW-NW.

  At 20:20, winds at Henninger Flats Road shifted, blowing from ESE. They shifted again
  at 20:30, blowing ENE before returning to NNE at 20:40. At Rubio Canyon, winds shifted,
  blowing from the west at 20:30, then from SSE at 20:40 before returning to NNW at 20:50. At
  20:30, these weather stations reported winds blowing in nearly opposite directions in this area.

  Additionally, the reversal of winds at 20:40 at Rubio Canyon could explain why LAC units that
  were prepared to address the approaching flames on North Altadena Drive and Pinecrest
  Drive later encountered embers blowing over their position.

  This fire behavior was consistent with the erratic “mountain wave,” or horizontal roll vortices,
  that were forecasted for the western slopes (Lee side) of the San Gabriel Mountains.

  At 21:30, the wind direction at Henninger Flats Road shifted from NNE-NE to blowing from the
  south at 21:30, then SSW at 21:40. For the remainder of the night, winds were reported from
  SW-SSW at this location.

                                                       Also at this time, the local temperature at
                                                       the weather station changed dramatically,
                                                       increasing from 56°F at 21:10 to 64°F at
                                                       21:20, peaking to 87°F at 21:30 (when the
                                                       winds were blowing from the south). This
                                                       shift in wind direction, while fire was still
                                                       burning around Kinneloa Mesa, may have
                                                       enhanced the transport of embers and
                                                       spread of the flames into the mountains to
                                                       the NNE.

  Weather Conditions
  Temperature profiles in urban areas that were plotted in Weather Conditions sidebars
  throughout this section are overlaid in the graph below, showing the westward progression of
  thermal impacts on these weather stations.

  As the fire initially pushed south, temperatures at the Eaton Canyon weather station
  began to increase around 19:00. Westward impacts were first noted at the Rubio Canyon
  (Longitude:-118.12309) and the East Palm Street (Longitude:-118.12523) weather stations at
  around 23:00 when sustained increases in air temperatures were consistently reported.

  At approximately 02:00 on
  January 8, 2025, temperatures at
  the East Loma Alta Drive weather

January 7, 2025
Note: Timestamps marked with (*) denote approximate times shared by individuals during TDs. Timestamps
marked with (#) were taken from AVL data and may not be included in the master timeline.

 10:30     Fire first reported by a 911 call to LA Metro. The caller, from near 11** Piedra Morada Drive,
           reports a “huge fire across the hills from us in our back yard.”

 10:30     University of California San Diego ALERTCalifornia cameras detect smoke above Skull Rock in
           the same general area as the 911 caller’s report. LFD and LAC units are working at a brush fire
           (Sunset 1 Fire) in the Hollywood Hills. The Sunset 1 Fire is quickly brought under control, so units
           still en route to the Sunset 1 Fire are reassigned to the Palisades Fire.

 10:31     LA Metro dispatch the following LFD units to the Palisades Fire:
           • One (1) BC
           • LF69, LF63, LF37
           • E23, E69, E19, E59, E292
           • RA23
           • EMS9
           • Fire 1, Fire 2, Fire 3, Fire 4 (HLCO) LFD helicopters working at the Sunset 1 Fire are added to
             the Palisades Fire

 10:32     LFD ST 1880C is added to the Palisades Fire.

 10:33     LF69, observing the smoke while en route, orders all LFD helicopters and 10 additional engine
           companies.

 10:35*    An LFD AC, working in LA Metro, determines the fire would likely impact the LAC SRA and
           requests immediate assistance. LFD AC contacts LA FCCF on the radio and requests a first
           alarm assignment, 20 Engines, and three (3) Type 3 fixed-wing aircraft. Figure 4.3.137 illustrates
           the perspective of the LAC and LFD response zones in relation to the Palisades Fire perimeter.

 10:35*    An LAC AC responds when he receives a PulsePoint108 notification of the Palisades Fire, and
           sees the smoke on the ALERTCalifornia camera in his office.

 10:39     At LA Metro’s request, LA FCCF dispatches the following LAC first alarm brush assignment:
           • Two (2) LAC BCs
           • LAC E70, E65, E67, E68, E69, E88, E125
           • LAC P70, P69, P65
           • Four (4) LAC SUs; Crews 8-2, 8-3, 8-4, 13-1; Dozer Team 5
           • LAC Copter 11, Copter 17, Copter 18, QRF-HT55, Quebec 1 & 2,
           • LAC WT70
           • LAC SQ88

           Many LAC chief officers start moving towards the Palisades Fire with the expectation that the fire
           would soon impact the county-responsibility area.


                                                      245
4.0 TIMELINE OF EVENTS                                                                         PALISADES




10:43     The first responding LFD BC, while en route, requests an additional 10 engine companies for a
          total of 20 engines.

10:43     LFD LF69, while en route, states over the radio: “10 acres in medium to heavy brush, with about
          a forty mile an hour wind blowing towards the ocean.”

10:44     Palisades Incident Command (IC) is established, as shown in Figure 4.3.135 and Table 4.3.7.
          Initial incident objectives are set and the new VHF Palisades Incident Communication Plan is
          communicated over the radio. The IC begins making resource assignments.

10:46     HLCO follows with an additional report: “This thing is already at two (2) acres. It is
          100% in alignment with the wind. It started at a ridge top and pushed directly towards the
          Palisades. This has the potential for 200 acres in the next 20 minutes, we probably have
          an impact time into structures being threatened in under 20 minutes.”

          Palisades IC announces the ICP location at LFD FS23 and establishes the initial staging location
          for the Palisades incident on Palisades Drive and West Sunset Boulevard.

10:48     Radio Size-Up Report states: “Metro from LF69 on scene, the Palisades incident
          named as Palisades IC, we have approximately 30 acres of medium to heavy brush
          burning towards the ocean. Keep all companies coming.”

          *SDG2 consists of LFD LF69, LFD E23, and LFD E19 began pulling hand lines between six (6)
          to seven (7) homes and defending structures on Chastain Parkway. Backing fire moves towards
          structures.

          LFD E23 and other LFD resources are positioned for structure defense close to 18** Chastain
          Parkway in the Highlands neighborhood and also assist with evacuations.

          An LAC BC is dispatched and responds to the ICP at LFD FS23, and becomes LAC’s first AREP.

10:49     Palisades IC establishes the LFD Helispot at Santa Ynez Reservoir and is notified that LAC is
          setting up Helispot 69B.

10:50*    An LAC DC responds when he sees the smoke on his ALERTCalifornia camera in his office and
          after having a conversation with his boss.

11:00     LFD recalls off-duty personnel.

11:03#*   LAC AC arrives on scene at Gladstones Restaurant:
          • Instructs the LAC OEM Director and the LAC Division 7 OEM staff to meet him at Gladstones.
          • Uses Tablet Command (TC) to initiate evacuation notices for Topanga Zone 7 and 9, Mesa
            Zone 10, and later, for Malibu Zone 111 and 112.
          • Begins to build a command structure with other LAC chief officers using LAC LA-RICS Fire 7
            channel, in anticipation of the fire’s arrival into the county-responsibility area.
          • Discusses moving the ICP location.




                                                    246
4.0 TIMELINE OF EVENTS                                                                        PALISADES




11:20    Two LFD Command officers (DC and AC) arrive at FS23.

         HLCO reports the fire now at 200 acres and Palisades IC updated LA Metro.

11:25    LA Metro notifies Palisades IC that senior staff at LFD headquarters want to issue an evacuation
         warning for the Palisades area.

         Spot fires are reported near the staging area:
         • Due to gridlocked traffic, fire companies could not respond up Palisades Drive to the Palisades
           Highlands neighborhood.
         • Fire companies in Staging were requested for structure defense and evacuation assistance
           near West Sunset Boulevard and Palisades Drive.

11:27    LFD E94 reports to Palisades IC that homes are being impacted by fire on Charmel and Lachman
         Lanes and requests more resources.

         Radio reports of the fire spotting on the west side of Palisades Drive and moving south west
         come in.

11:28    An LAC AC announces to all Palisades resources that the Palisades Incident was in Unified
         Command.
         • An LFD AC assumes the Palisades IC.
         • The LFD BC who was operating as IC becomes the Operations Section Chief (OSC).
         • An LFD DC becomes the Agency Administrator.


11:29    LFD Deputy IC confirms with LA Metro and issues an evacuation order for the Palisades area
         down to PCH. LFD Deputy IC notifies Palisades OSC of the need for LAPD to get traffic cleared
         on Palisades Drive.

11:30    Radio traffic to the OSC from the Deputy IC: “We need to talk to PD and get all this traffic
         on Palisades Drive freeflowed out to PCH, the fire has spotted on both sides of Palisades Drive,
         and we need to get these cars out of here immediately.”

         *Camp 8 crews were working to secure the heel of the fire by constructing a fire line at the area
         of origin. Crews work in the area until 17:30 without assistance from engine companies.

11:40    LFD Deputy IC establishes the Evacuation Branch with LAPD.

11:45*   An LAC AC & Lost Hills Command Officer direct LAC Community Brigade (II) and LASD deputies
         to begin evacuating the community of Big Rock.

11:53    ICP moves to Gladstones Restaurant at West Sunset Boulevard and PCH.

11:54    Palisades IC updates the control objectives; keep the fire east of Carbon Canyon, north of PCH,
         west of Rustic Canyon, and south of Mulholland.

12:00    Federal contract air tankers over the fire.



                                                       247
4.0 TIMELINE OF EVENTS                                                                        PALISADES




12:12#   LAC STs 1870C, 1151C, and 1103A move from their prepositioned areas throughout the county
         to Topanga Canyon Boulevard and PCH. In a short period of time, most LAC ST resources are
         deployed into the Castellamare neighborhood.

12:15    Ordering point transfers from LFD to LAC due to fire progressing toward LAC direct protection
         area (i.e. jurisdictional response area).

12:16*   Additional command actions:
         • Division M is established under supervision of an LAC AC which includes Sunset Mesa and the
           Getty Villa.
         • Coastline SDG is established.
         • A firefighter mayday is cleared.
         • A large resource order is placed.
         • A SMA DC is assigned as the Planning Section Chief (PSC).
         • An LFD BC is assigned as the Evacuation Branch Director.
         • An LFD Captain II is assigned as the Liaison officer

         Although LAC & LFD are in Unified Command and there continues to be some coordination
         between the two (2) agencies, tactical operations are managed as two (2) separate incidents, based
         upon each agency’s geographical areas of responsibility (LFD Branch V and LAC Branch VII).

12:49    It is reported that the hillsides adjacent to the staging area are burning and LFD TF1010 and
         LFD TF1012 are assigned to conduct operations at the adjacent strip mall at Palisades Drive and
         West Sunset Boulevard.

12:54    Coastline Staging is established at Temescal Canyon Road and PCH at Will Rogers State Beach
         for resources arriving from the east.

13:05    Palisades IC establishes Division C. By this time, the Incident Organization had four (4) Divisions
         (A, C, M, and Z) and four (4) SDGs (SDG1, SDG2, SDG3, and Coastline SDG).

         Palisades IC establishes a Palisades Medical Group.

13:08    LFD EMS9 at Sunset Staging requests “Immediate evacuation right now, we have a life hazard,
         people are blocked in their cars, and we are evacuating on foot.” Civilian and emergency
         vehicles are in gridlock and civilians are advised to evacuate on foot. They are assisted by fire
         and police personnel.

13:20*   LASD and LAC Community Brigade (II) are in the Big Rock neighborhoods knocking &
         notifying people to leave. LASD units remain in the area for the next 5 hours.

         Federal and State fixed wing aircraft dropping water and retardant until 17:00.

13:46    OSC announces the incident priorities over the command channel of life safety and evacuation.
         OSC notifies the divisions to break down the SDGs and absorb them into the division organization.

14:03    Radio traffic states that Palisades Drive and West Sunset Boulevard are impassable with 100
         abandoned vehicles, and a request is made for heavy equipment to move them.

4.0 TIMELINE OF EVENTS                                                                        PALISADES




14:10#   LAC resources are assigned tactical patrol of Getty Villa property through the early morning of
         January 8, 2025.

14:35    LAC Dozer Team 5 begins clearing vehicles to open access to Palisades Drive. This process
         lasts for 45 minutes with access eventually provided to the Highlands neighborhood.

14:45    The ICP is relocated for a second time to Will Rogers State Beach parking lot, on the ocean side
         of PCH, approximately ⅓ mile east of Temescal Canyon Road.

16:00*   LFD E19 defends structures along Palsair Place in El Medio Bluffs using hydrant and pool water.
         E19 is re-located to LFD LF69’s location in the Alphabet Streets near LFD FS69.

16:10    An LAC DC assumes Unified IC for LAC.

16:25    Fire is reported west of Topanga Canyon Boulevard. Resources are requested to approach from
         the west.

16:30    Staging area is established at Las Flores and PCH for resources arriving from the west.

         *LAC Community Brigade (I) engages with LAC and other agencies in Sunset Mesa. They
         witness residents using pool pumps to defend homes in the area.

16:40#   As the fire progresses to the west, LAC resources defend structures in Castellamare, Getty
         Villa / Pacific View Estates, and Sunset Mesa neighborhoods. Most LAC resources that are
         staged at Topanga Canyon and PCH (with a few exceptions) are in LAC’s responsibility area
         (Sunset Mesa and west) by 16:40.

16:48#   LFD resources, including E33, E39, E82C, and E95, respond to the Tahitian Terrace mobile home
         park. Resources are in the mobile home park until approximately 18:40.

17:00*   LAC ST 1870C and other units experience near blackout conditions in Sunset Mesa. They spend
         several hours attempting to save structures, at times having to move engines to a safe location to
         prevent being burned.

17:14#   LFD TF69 proceeds to the area of 11** El Medio Drive where several homes are on fire with no
         fire resources. Fire is spreading from house to house with estimated 50-60 mph winds. The unit
         makes a stand at El Medio Drive and Anoka Place for four (4) hours to protect approximately 18
         homes in the area of El Medio Avenue, Anoka Drive, and Palisair Drive (El Medio Bluffs).

17:33*   Many structure fire calls are received by LA Metro as the Palisades Fire progresses. LA Metro
         deploys over 30 engines and additional companies into the area, principally within the Palisades
         Village and along Sunset Boulevard. They dispatch multiple full-structure assignments, including
         several apartment fires, within the fire’s footprint.

         *Firefighters responding to these separate incidents operate with minimal coordination and
         limited awareness of the surrounding fire activity.

17:59    Radio report from a private fire resource (Red Zone 2) to OSC indicates that several structures
         are being threatened on Radcliffe Avenue in the Via Bluffs neighborhood with only two (2) private
         fire resources in the area.




                                                   249
4.0 TIMELINE OF EVENTS                                                                        PALISADES




18:21    Division S requests resources for the Big Rock community.

18:22    Radio communications indicate evacuation had been addressed by law enforcement in Big Rock.

18:27    Division S reports that the fire would impact the Big Rock area in approximately 20 minutes.
         Division S assigns TF12 to Big Rock to evaluate structure defense possibilities. TF12 determines
         this area to be undefendable under the current and expected conditions.

18:30*   Division U responds from LAC FS69 to the coast via Topanga Canyon Boulevard. As he drives
         towards PCH, driving conditions deteriorate significantly. Downed powerlines, embercast,
         extreme wind, and increasing fire activity force him to find an alternate route to the coast.

         Division U and other responders scout the inland side of PCH, including the community of Big
         Rock. They determine that due to the current conditions and forecast, placing resources any
         considerable distance from PCH would be ineffective and dangerous.

18:30*   Just after the Eaton Fire starts in Altadena at 18:18, six LAC chiefs meet at LAC Helispot 69B in
         the Santa Monica Mountains to discuss the current Palisades incident.Two of these chief officers
         who were assigned TF3 and Operations Branch Director (OPBD) VII, leave these assignments
         at the Palisades Fire and respond to the Eaton Fire, implementing the plan LAC had developed
         in the morning. First arriving LAC AC that was initially part of the unified command structure
         becomes Branch VII OPBD.

18:59    Division Z reports the hydrants are dry in the area of Glenhaven Drive in the Marquez Knolls and
         Ridgeview Country Estates neighborhoods. Multiple LFD resources continue to work in the area
         until 22:00.

19:00    VNC TF12 scouts Big Rock to find multiple homes well-involved and determines access for Type 1
         engines isn’t possible. Two (2) Type 6 engines also scout the area and find challenging conditions.

         LFD LF63 attaches to LFD ST1055A which is engaged in structure defense in the Santa Ynez,
         Temescal Canyon Road, Marquez Elementary School, Lachman Lane, and Sunset Boulevard
         areas (Marquez Knolls and Ridgeview Country Estates).

19:01    Radio report from a private fire resource (Red Zone 2) to OSC indicates at least eight (8)
         structures are now on fire in the Via Bluffs neighborhood. Resources on scene are the LAC
         Community Brigade and a private Type 6 resource.

19:15    HLCO grounds all aircraft due to high winds and turbulent conditions.

19:18#   LFD E33, E39, E95, and E82C respond to the Via Bluffs neighborhood. They are assisted by a
         ST from Long Beach. They conduct suppression activities in the neighborhood until 23:15.

19:20    Multiple LFD resources, including E205, E295, and SQ95, conduct suppression of several
         burning buildings at Palisades High School. Resources stay in the high school for several hours.

19:48    The ICP moves for the final time to the Zuma State Beach parking lot. The ICP remains at Zuma
         Beach until completion of the incident.

20:00*   LFD E23 experiences loss of hydrant pressure in the Highlands/Summit area.

          foundations and find people who require assistance evacuating.

22:00     Very low hydrant pressure near LFD FS69 and the Alphabet Streets.

22:29     A civilian sustains a severe burn injury near PCH and Big Rock Drive.

22:30*#   LFD E23, one of the first engines on scene of the fire, stay in the Highlands area on tactical patrol.

22:32*    LAC P75 is also dispatched as a single resource to a call for service in the Big Rock community.
          He stated that upon arrival the fire had passed the neighborhood, burning many homes. Division S
          later assigned a USFS-Type 6 ST to patrol the area.

22:54     Above ground electrical/communications lines down across PCH near Duke’s restaurant, blocking
          resources.

January 8, 2025

 00:00*    LAC Camp 8 personnel estimates wind speeds at the south east portion of the camp to be close
           to 100 mph just prior to fire impacting the facility.

 00:36     Branch I reports to OSC that the eastern edge of the fire is in the Riviera neighborhood off of
           Umeo Road with structures actively burning.

 01:00*    LAC firefighters defend LAC Camp 8 at the top of the west facing slope of Las Flores Canyon as
           fire impacts the south east section of camp. Civilians shelter in a kitchen with firefighters. Fascias
           are torn off multiple structures and firefighters extinguish fires throughout the facility.

 01:11*#   LFD resources, including LFD E14, E62, and E221, respond to the vicinity of Umeo Road.
           They are joined by other LFD resources and have good success in the Riviera neighborhood.
           Resources stay in this neighborhood until the afternoon of January 8, 2025.

 02:30     LADWP deploys 46 water tenders.

 05:00*    LAC Firefighters defend the north end of LAC Camp 8 as the fire runs up the slope near the
           offices just off of Las Flores Canyon Road and Rambla Pacifico Street.

 06:00*    After fire blew through Camp 8, LAC firefighters at the camp assist local civilians searching
           nearby homes for victims. Camp 8 crewmembers continue fire suppression around the camp.

 07:38*#   LAC Crew Superintendent responds to a report of a person who stayed in his cottage at 17**
           Rambla Pacifico Street. The cottage had burned to the ground. Driving in the area is treacherous
           with downed power lines throughout the neighborhoods around Camp 8.

 09:00*    Day shift briefing at Zuma Beach ICP with CDF team.

           By the morning of January 8, 2025, the fire had spread east as far as Rustic Canyon and
           Sunset Boulevard. The fire resources continue to engage and fight fire wherever they
           find an opportunity to save property.

4.3.2.2.1 Jurisdictional Agencies: Eaton
The start of the Eaton Fire generated multiple calls for service which, as a result, produced two (2) dispatch
locations. These two (2) locations were positioned in neighboring jurisdictions and created a multi-agency
response, including LAC, PAS and ANF. Figure 4.3.138 provides a geographical reference of these jurisdictions
and fire stations in relation to the Eaton Fire.

        • ANF E328, E310, E313, E334, E512, E321, E23
        • ANF WT255, WT34
        • ANF Crew 4 (The Little Tujunga Hotshots)

18:22   A PAS BC establishes the Eaton Fire initial staging location at North Altadena Drive and New
        York Drive. A staging area manager (STAM) is not assigned as resources are not staged, and
        instead are being immediately deployed into the incident.

18:23   LAC units dispatched on a nearby structure fire cancel their response to the structure fire and are
        placed on the brush fire, replacing many of the original dispatched resources.

18:24   The modified LAC first alarm response was:
        • Three (3) LAC BCs
        • LAC E5, E11, E12, E19, E63, E66, E82, WT32
        • LAC Copter 11, Copter 17
        • LAC Dozer Team 6
        • LAC Camp Crews 19-1, 19-2, 12-4, 14-2
        • Two (2) LAC SUs
        • LAC P66 and S19

        The dispatch address for the brush fire comes in at two (2) addresses: 22**
        Pinecrest Drive for LAC and ANF resources and 21** Canyon Close Road for PAS resources.

        Due to multiple dispatches, the county resources that responded to the structure fire are assigned
        a structure fire communications plan, while PAS is operating on a brush fire communications
        plan. This led to an initial delay in situational awareness due to the LAC BC not hearing the initial
        Pasadena brush fire radio traffic.




                                                   267
4.0 TIMELINE OF EVENTS                                                                          EATON




18:26    Radio size-up and initial ICP is established at the Eaton Canyon Equestrian Center.

         “All units responding to the brush fire up in Eaton Canyon… we’re on scene. We have an
         approximately 10 acre brush fire, across [the] canyon, underneath high tension power lines.
         Command post location is set up at the Eaton Canyon Equestrian Park on New York, just east
         of Altadena.”

         Responding resources were either assigned to an area by the PAS BC, or self deployed to an
         area and reported their location to the IC.

         The first arriving LAC resources respond to Pinecrest Drive, northwest of the area of origin. The
         first arriving PAS resources respond to Canyon Close Road, just southeast of the area of origin.

         These initial resource assignments are made based on the reported address of the brush fire,
         individual fire companies direct observation, by HLCO fire progression reports, or based on the
         expected fire movement due to wind and terrain.

18:30*   LAC E5, immediately upon arrival on Canyon Close Road, assists LASD with evacuation in the
         neighborhood. Reports extreme embercast that is burning holes in brush Personal Protective
         Equipment (PPE).

18:35    IC sends resources into various areas around Eaton Canyon for situational awareness and then
         makes assignments based on the information received.

18:45*   The fire is pushing down Eaton Canyon to the south, skirting homes on the west side of the
         canyon and beginning to impact the Nature Center, the Kinneloa Canyon neighborhood, and the
         neighborhoods near New York Drive and North Altadena Drive.

18:49    LAC Chief Officers are reassigned from the Palisades Fire to the Eaton Fire.

18:56    HLCO recommends the IC issue evacuations in Genasys map area PAS-109.

19:00*   Multiple additional single-resource apparatus and overhead resources arrive on scene, or are
         operating in the area and not assigned to the incident. The IC assigns some of these resources
         to various locations on New York Drive and North Altadena Drive. Many of these resources use
         TIER (Take Initiative, Engage, Report) to direct their actions in the absence of direct supervision.

19:02    LAC E169 is assigned to the Eaton Nature Center. They pull out when it was overrun by fire.

19:06    The ICP is moved to Farnsworth Park and an LAC BC assumes IC. The original IC (PAS BC)
         becomes the new OSC.

19:11    An LAC BC is assigned as the supervisor of the New York SDG (in the area of New York Drive
         and North Altadena Drive) and gathers up resources in this area to form the group, including LAC
         E169, an ANF BC, ANF E321, and ANF WT225.




                                                    268
4.0 TIMELINE OF EVENTS                                                                              EATON




19:15*#   The PAS BC acting as OSC and an LAC AC met in person near the intersection of Allen Avenue
          and East Washington Boulevard, behind a Wells Fargo Bank, to complete transfer of command to
          the LAC AC.

          At this time, it is recognized that the current incident organization is being quickly outpaced by the
          fire’s progression from brush into the neighborhoods, with house-to-house ignition happening on
          a massive scale. The LAC AC assumes radio communications as OSC, while both chiefs and an
          assistant attempt to reconcile resources and begin to build a new incident organization.

          The LAC OSC reorganizes the incident from a SDG organization to branch and division
          management.

19:18     HLCO recommends the OSC issue evacuations in Genasys map area LAC E-183.

          HLCO returns to Barton Helibase due to high winds. (All aircraft are grounded until early
          afternoon of January 8, 2025.)

19:20     The IC makes the first radio transmission communicating the incident priorities: “We are in life
          priority mode, keep units mobile and do not anchor in, all focus is on life priority.”

19:27     Multiple units are on fire at the Creekside condominiums located at Dove Creek Lane and
          Creekside Court in Pasadena.

19:30*    The fire spreads rapidly along east Altadena and east of Eaton Canyon and supervisors reassign
          resources in the field between different SDGs, based on need.

          The initial incident assignments and organizational structure are in place and are summarized in
          Figure 4.3.139 and Table 4.3.12. (Eaton Initial Organization map and table, in Section 4.3.2.2.3).

          Fire companies go to work preparing structures for fire impact, assisting with evacuations and
          conducting fire suppression activities. These tasks continue for the rest of the night on January
          7, 2025.

19:55*    Engines from ST1112A defending structures within the Creekside condominium complex.
          Sixteen structures burn, but several are saved. Engines remain in the community until
          approximately 01:40.

20:03     An urgent call for resources is transmitted for fire impacting an assisted living facility.
          • LAC E54 and LAC E163 are assigned to respond to the assisted living facility reported to be at
            13** North Altadena Drive (actual address is 25** East Washington Boulevard).
          • An LAC BC assists in the management of the evacuation of the facility.

20:05     LAC E74 is assigned as STAM.

20:34     Discussing continued changes to the incident management organization to adapt to the escalating
          conditions.




                                                       269
4.0 TIMELINE OF EVENTS                                                                           EATON




 20:40*    Due to extremely difficult breathing conditions, LAC E5 dons breathing apparatus over their brush
           PPE while providing suppression activities in the area of New York Drive and Altadena Drive
           where multiple structures are on fire.

 20:52     ICP is relocated for a third time to the Rose Bowl at parking lots K and L in Pasadena. In the
           early morning hours of January 8, 2025, although the Rose Bowl is being impacted by significant
           smoke, the ICP remains at this location until completion of the incident.

 21:01     Radio reports of homes burning with no fire engines in the area are reported at the top of Villa
           Heights Road (Kinneloa Mesa).

 22:25     For resources arriving from the east, OSC creates a second staging location (Baldwin staging) at
           North Baldwin Avenue and Sierra Madre Boulevard. No STAM was assigned to Baldwin Staging
           over the radio and few resources ended up being staged. For resources arriving from the west,
           the initial staging area is renamed New York Staging. Many en route resources contacted OSC
           directly on the radio for an assignment. These units are assigned to a division over the radio and
           bypassed staging.

 22:30*    The LAC OSC finalizes the new incident organization consisting of Branch VII with Divisions Z
           and M west of Altadena Drive, and Branch III with Divisions A and D east of Altadena Drive. An
           LAC DC becomes the Unified IC at the Rose Bowl.

 23:13     Radio report from the field to OSC by a fire department Safety Officer of homes burning at the top
           of Sierra Meadow Drive (Sierra Madre) and requesting resources.

 23:16     Division D reports to the OSC that homes are burning with not enough fire engines in the area at
           the top of Oak Crest Drive (Sierra Madre).

 23:40     Report over the radio from LA FCCF to OSC of a fire west of Lake Avenue at 5** East Calaveras
           Street (One structure burned on this block) (Central Altadena).

 23:55     Report over the radio of a fire at 5** East Calaveras Street (No structures burned on this block in
           Central Altadena).


January 8, 2025

 00:30*#   LAC E12 conducts fire suppression on Porter Avenue and East Altadena Drive. Numerous
           structures burning and deteriorating conditions.

 00:54     Field personnel report the fire continuing to push to the east and advise OSC to continue to send
           resources to the east. OSC continues to make Branch III (east of Eaton Canyon) the priority for
           resource assignments.

 00:57     Radio transmission of a fire at 5** East Las Flores Drive. (Nearly every home in this
           neighborhood burned). OSC contacts the DIVS requesting resources for the west side of the fire.

           By now, the fire footprint has rapidly expanded to a seven (7) mile wide area with destruction far
           outpacing available resources and overwhelming suppression efforts. Several STs and single
           resources require reassignment from Division A to Division Z.



                                                     270
4.0 TIMELINE OF EVENTS                                                                             EATON




01:05*#   ST1112A fighting numerous structure fires at the top of Hastings Ranch Drive and vicinity.

01:14     Resources respond back to the Creekside condominiums to suppress fire from re-ignition.

01:15*#   LAC E12 moved up to Stonehill Drive and East Alta Loma Drive with multiple structures on fire.
          The unit conducts suppression and assists with door-to-door evacuation notifications with LASD.

01:58*#   DIVS Z travels to Lake Avenue via East Altadena Drive to investigate the extent of fire spread
          and finds intense fire conditions that worsen as he attempts to travel to Fair Oaks Avenue. He
          could not continue as he encountered zero visibility, intense heat, and had serious concerns of
          becoming trapped.

02:00*    OSC receives a phone call that indicates a major threat to the community. It was imperative
          that the NASA Jet Propulsion Laboratory (JPL) be protected due to the possible catastrophic
          implications to civilian life if the facility was impacted by the fire.

02:11     LAC E74 was reassigned from STAM to Division A due to the urgent need for any resources.

02:18     OSC assigns LAC TF91 and LAC E63 to pre-plan JPL. Once the facility was pre-planned, these
          resources are re-assigned to Division Z as there was no immediate threat to JPL.

02:35     Significant shift in reports of fire activity from the east side of Lake Avenue to the west side of
          Lake Avenue.

03:30*#   LAC E12 relocates back to Pinecrest Drive (where they were assigned during the initial attack) to
          suppress spot fires that threatened homes that they had success defending earlier.

03:58*#   LAC E5 relocates to 13** Medford Road and prevents a fully involved house fire from extending
          to other surrounding houses in the neighborhood.

04:53*#   LAC E66 is reassigned to Division Z from Hastings Ranch and encounters large fires and
          extremely difficult working conditions in the area of El Molino Avenue and Fontanet Way. Later
          assists in the evacuation of the assisted living facility at 22** El Molino Avenue.

05:43     A report of three (3) persons trapped at 30** North Fair Oaks Avenue (Janes Village) is conveyed
          to OSC. Division Z reports back that they attempted to get to the location, but could not due to
          fire activity.

06:05     An urgent request is made to OSC for resources to assist with a very large assisted living facility
          at 22** El Molino Avenue (near Lake Avenue). PAS assists with getting buses to the location for
          evacuation. The OSC does not reply to the request over the radio, however, four (4) engines and
          a patrol resource assists with the life safety request.

06:14     An additional report states three (3) persons trapped at 30** North Fair Oaks Avenue (Janes
          Village). LAC E34 attempts to get to the location, but only makes it to the intersection of West
          Altadena Drive and North Fair Oaks Avenue at 06:24, several blocks short of the reported address.

06:38     TF1860A reports to Division D that hydrants were running dry.




                                                      271
4.0 TIMELINE OF EVENTS                                                                               EATON




 06:55*#    ST1112A relocates to West Altadena Drive and notes heavy fire conditions and near-zero visibility
            with downed power lines everywhere. Once in the area north of West Woodbury Drive along
            Lincoln Avenue south of West Altadena Drive, the unit checks in with Division Z, and along with

January 7, 2025
Note: Timestamps marked with (*) denote approximate times shared by individuals during TDs. Timestamps
marked with (#) were taken from AVL data and may not be included in the master timeline.

 10:23     LAPD (AAR p.10) - The first fire-related radio call is generated by the Communications Division.
           Incident No. 25010700001693 at 12** Piedra Morada Drive in the Pacific Palisades Highlands.
           Minutes later, another radio call is generated at 165** Calle Victoria, also in the Highlands
           neighborhood. These calls were not broadcast on West LA base frequency.

 10:24     LAPD (AAR p.11) - A West LA XL [XL-additional patrol vehicle with single person staffing] unit
           notices several fire engines driving code-three (3) towards the Highlands. The unit inquired about
           a possible fire with Communications. Communications confirmed a report of a fire. The officer
           informed Communications that he would respond to the fire’s location. Upon arriving at the scene,
           the officer assessed the size and intensity of the fire and requested three (3) additional units to
           respond code-three.

 10:40*    LASD - EOB personnel are at Los Angeles International Airport (LAX) and can see smoke in the
           hills above Pacific Palisades. They respond to the LFD ICP as liaisons.

 10:53     LAPD (AAR p.48) - LFD notifies Communications to advise responding LAPD units that an ICP
           has been set up at FS23, 172** Sunset Boulevard, down the hill from the Palisades Highlands.

 11:00*    LASD - SIB sends a PIO to the LAPD ICP to liaison and ensure a unified message to the public.

 11:05     LAPD (AAR p.48) - A West LA supervisor, while enroute to the unit’s location, spots smoke and
           stops at the intersection of Via Floresta and Floresta Place. He requests additional units to
           respond code-two (2) for evacuations.

 11:15     LAPD (AAR p.11) - 45 minutes after the first radio calls was generated, the West LA sergeant at
           the top of Ridgeview decides that the size of the fire and its proximity to nearby homes presents
           a massive threat to public safety, leading him to declare a Citywide Tactical Alert and request 12
           additional units.

 11:17     LAPD (AAR p.48) - Air Units are unable to respond due to the weather.

 11:19     LAPD (AAR p.48) - Units are requested for traffic control at PCH and Sunset to block north/south
           traffic and to Palisades and Sunset.

 11:20     LAPD (AAR p.48) - Communications Radio Telephone Operation (RTO) broadcasts the Citywide
           Tac Alert.

 11:24     LAPD (AAR p.48) - A tactical frequency is requested to facilitate clearer communication amongst
           officers during the evacuation. All officers assigned to the incident are instructed to switch to
           Citywide Tac 2, Channel 40.

 11:27     LAPD (AAR p.49)- West LA supervisor requests that the ICP coordinate with LFD and provide
           an evacuation plan. The supervisor requests that responding units respond to evacuation areas
           instead of the ICP.

5.0 SUMMARY


This report is the first of two (2) phases of an                   Losses of life and property occurred despite the
independent study commissioned by the Office of                    herculean efforts of ordinary people, neighbors,
the Governor of California, and conducted by the UL                and front-line first responders, who — despite the
Research Institutes (ULRI), to examine the tragic                  extremely dangerous conditions — were still able to
fires that burned through Los Angeles and Ventura                  save countless lives, homes, and businesses.
Counties in January 2025. The January fires’ origin
and cause investigations are not within the scope of               Simply stated, the Phase One report describes
ULRI’s charge; those analyses are being performed by               what happened, when, and where – based on data
the local authorities having jurisdiction, with assistance         acquired at the time of publication — with emphasis
from state, federal, and other forensic fire specialists.          on the most severe urban conflagrations, the
                                                                   Palisades and Eaton Fires, and the initial period of
In this Phase One review, ULRI’s experienced                       this multi-day fire event with an ongoing recovery.
fire safety research team provided background
information relevant to both reports, described the                ULRI’s Phase Two report will provide a
rapid fire spread that characterized the Palisades and             comprehensive assessment of these major fire
Eaton Fires’ progress through the built environment,               incidents occurring in January 2025, including the
and constructed an evidence-based timeline of the                  “why” and “how” of these complex,
fire and emergency services’ collective response to                multifaceted events.
the most consequential urban fire storm in Southern
California history.                                                This independent analysis will include findings and
                                                                   recommendations addressing each component
Comparing the fire progression and response                        of the prevention, preparedness, and response
timelines, it appears that the responding fire and                 systems required to address wildfire initiated urban
emergency service agencies in this area of Southern                conflagrations. Phase Two will also update the
California were overwhelmed by the number of                       background, timelines, and findings presented
concurrent fires; the fires’ severity; the rapid, wind-            in Phase One — if necessary — based on new
driven fire spread through the built environment; and              information received from cooperating organizations
the resulting resource limitations.                                since the publication of this first report.

Despite agencies taking critical steps to prepare                  In closing, the entire ULRI team conveys our
for the severe fire weather that was forecasted well               deepest sympathies to all those affected by the
in advance of early January 2025, having access                    January 2025 fires in Southern California and
to a response system that provides extensive                       reiterates our charge to provide an independent,
fire suppression resources drawn from multiple                     objective, and comprehensive review based on the
agencies, and deploying one of the largest initial                 best-available data, research, and safety science.
attack responses to date in the United States,
Southern California experienced massive loss of life
and property.

complex web of federal, regional, state, local agencies, and NGOs that shape wildfire mitigation, preparedness,
and recovery efforts.

6.0 APPENDIX


6.2 Incident Business Management Agreements (Section 2.5.5)

Table 6.2.1 California Cost Reconciliation Agreements and Methods.



                      California Fire Cost Reconciliation Agreements and Methods

          Agreement/Method                                                                  Description
 California Master Mutual Aid Agreement     An agreement between the State and its various political subdivisions for provision of an incremental
 (Master Mutual Aid)                        and progressive system of mobilization of Statewide emergency resources on a reciprocal basis,
                                            the agreement facilitates the sharing of resources during emergencies and establishes the legal and
                                            financial mechanisms for those actions. Typically, response involves a request for assistance on an ad
                                            hoc basis

 California Fire Assistance Agreement-      The CFAA is the negotiated reimbursement mechanism and standards for response agreement for
 (CFAA)                                     local government fire agency and Cal OES resources providing response through the California Fire
                                            Service and Rescue Emergency Mutual Aid System to fires within State (CAL FIRE) and Federal direct
                                            protection responsibility, providing reimbursement of response costs under qualifying conditions and
                                            circumstances

 California Master Cooperative Wildland     An agreement between CAL FIRE and the Federal wildfire agencies (USDA Forest Service, USDI
 Fire Management Agreement and Stafford     National Park Service, Fish and Wildlife Service, and Bureau of Indian Affairs) guiding cooperative
 Act Response Agreement –(CFMA)             response and cost reimbursement for agency fire resources providing service to State and Federal
                                            direct protection responsibility lands.

 CAL FIRE Contract County Agreement         An agreement between the State of California and the Counties of Kern, Los Angeles, Marin, Orange,
 (Contract County)                          Santa Barbara, and Ventura to provide direct protection of CAL FIRE jurisdictional responsibility lands
                                            (SRA) within their specific Counties, acting as an agent for CAL FIRE.

 FIRE MUTUAL ASSISTANCE GRANT               Administered by the Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA), the Fire Management
 –(FMAG)                                    Assistance Grant (FMAG) program is available to states, local and tribal governments, for the
                                            mitigation, management, and control of fires on publicly or privately owned forests or grasslands, which
                                            threaten such destruction as would constitute a major disaster, and provides a 75 percent Federal cost
                                            share for qualifying response costs.

 Federal Fire Severity Funding (Severity)   Federal Severity Funding allows agencies like the USDA-Forest Service to enhance their wildfire
                                            preparedness capabilities during periods of high or prolonged fire risk.

 Automatic Aid                              Automatic aid is a prearranged contractual agreement between fire departments to provide specific
                                            levels of routine assistance without requiring a specific request for each occurrence. Typically, service
                                            under such agreements is intended to be equal among participating parties.

 Preposition                                The strategic placement of firefighting resources in areas expected to be at higher risk during specific
                                            weather conditions or emergencies. This proactive measure aims to reduce response times and
                                            improve the efficiency of initial attack on wildfires or other incidents. In California, it is conducted via the
                                            terms of a legislative line item in the State Budget

 Assistance by Hire                         Resources contracted by a fire protection agency on a cost-plus reimbursement basis, for a specific
                                            rate and activity

 Source: Cal OES webpage
